The cohomology of real Grassmannians via Schubert stratifications
Eric Berry, Scotty Tilton

Abstract
In this paper, we present a closed formula for the cohomology of real Grassmannians. To achieve this, we use a theory of stratified spaces to compute the differentials in a chain complex that computes the cohomology. Specifically, we organize Schubert cells as a conically smooth stratified space in the sense of Ayala, Francis, Tanaka — the links therein yield the sought differentials, using methods in differential topology. Further, we identify the isomorphism type of this chain complex and we use this result to provide a closed formula for the additive structure of the cohomology of real Grassmannians with arbitrary coefficients.



1
Introduction

As the moduli space of
k
-dimensional subvector spaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the real Grassmannians,

\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, play an important role in the study of manifolds. Namely, we can interpret

H^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
as measuring obstructions to natural geometric questions about manifolds.
For instance, let
M\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}

be a
k
-dimensional submanifold of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Consider the Gauss map

\tau_{M}:M\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})~,\qquad x\mapsto T_{x}M~,

that sends a point
x\in M
to the tangent space of
M
at
x
. This induces a map on the level of cohomology

\tau_{M}^{*}:H^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))\to H^{*}(M)~.

For
M
a compact, orientable submanifold, there exists an element

e\in H^{k}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
such that
\tau_{M}(e)=0
if and only if
M
admits a non-vanishing vector field

[
MS75
]
.

To aid in the computation of the cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, there is a natural CW structure that one can place on the Grassmannian called the Schubert CW structure
[
MS75
]
. The complex Grassmannian,
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
, can also
be endowed with this structure. Since each cell in the Schubert CW strucutre of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n})
is even
dimensional, the boundary maps in the corresponding chain complex are zero. Thus,
H^{i}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{C}^{n}))

is freely generated by the Schubert cells of dimension
i

[
GH78
]
. A similar situation occurs in the
cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
with
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
coefficients. Namely, all the boundary maps in the
chain complex are zero. So again,
H^{i}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n});\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
is freely generated by the
Schubert cells of dimension
i

[
MS75
]
.
Borel computed the rational cohomology algebra of the odd dimensional real Grassmannians
[
Bor53
]
, and
Takeuchi computed the rational cohomology algebra of the even dimensional real Grassmannians
[
Tak62
]
.
More recently, equivariant versions of the rational cohomology have been considered in
[
Car16
]
,
[
He16
]
,

[
Sad17
]
.

We provide a complete description of the additive structure of the integral cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Fix
0\leq k\leq n
, and consider the poset

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}:=\text{Fun}^{\textsf{inj}}\bigl(\{1<\cdots<k\},\{1<\cdots<n\}\bigr)
of injective functors.
For each
0\leq r\leq k
, consider the map

d_{r}:\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\xrightarrow{\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}\mapsto\sum_{r\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-i}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}~.

In Section
4
, we prove the following:

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. The
R
-valued cohomology
\mathsf{H}^{\bullet}\bigl(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R\bigr)
is
isomorphic with the cohomology of the chain complex
\Bigl(R\left\langle\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\right\rangle,\delta\Bigr)
over
R
whose underlying graded
R
-module is free on the graded set

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and whose differential evaluates as

\delta:S=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}~\mapsto~\sum\limits_{r\in\left\{1\leq r\leq k~\mid~s_{r+1}-s_{r}>1\text{ and }k-s_{r}\text{ is odd}\right\}}(-1)^{d_{r-1}(S)}2\cdot S_{r}~,

(1)

where
S_{r}:=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{r-1}<s_{r}+1<s_{r+1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
.

In Section
5
we use Lemma
1.1
to provide the following remarkable
decomposition of
C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})
:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. There is an isomorphism of chain complexes

\displaystyle C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\otimes\mathsf{In}(S)}[d(S)-\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))]

\displaystyle\cong\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}\mathbb{Z}[d(S)]\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Min}}}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[d(S)-1]\right)

where
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}},\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}},\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Min}}\subset\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, and
\mathsf{In}(S)\subset\{1,\dots,k\}
.

A consequence of Theorem
1.2
is the following closed formula for the

R
-cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
:

Corollary 1.3
.

There is an isomorphism of graded
R
-modules

H^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}}V_{S}[d_{1}(S)]~,

where

V_{S}:=\begin{cases}R,&\text{ if }\mathsf{In}(S)=\emptyset=\mathsf{Out}(S)\\
\mathsf{ker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R),&\text{ if }\mathsf{Min}(\mathsf{In}(S)\cup\mathsf{Out}(S))\in\mathsf{Out}(S)\\
\mathsf{coker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R),&\text{ if }\mathsf{Min}(\mathsf{In}(S)\cup\mathsf{Out}(S))\in\mathsf{In}(S)\end{cases},

for
\mathsf{In}(S),\mathsf{Out}(S)\subset\{1,\dots,k\}
.

An immedaite consequence of Corollary
1.3
is the well known result:

Corollary 1.4
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring such that
R\xrightarrow{2}R
is the zero map, for instance
R=\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
,
then

H^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}}R[d_{1}(S)]~.

The difficulty in computing the integral cohomology of
real Grassmannians lies in the computation of
the attaching maps in the Schubert CW structure.
Using
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
coefficients, one is able to bypass this difficulty,
but to understand the integral cohomology, we must have a clear understanding of the attaching maps.
In
[
CK13
]
, the authors apply a
combinatorial approach via Young diagrams, and
in
[
Jun79
]
, Jungkind provides a closed formula for the differentials in the CW chain complex of

\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
with integer coefficients. Using our framework, we recover this formula as Lemma

1.1
above.

We now outline our approach, a novelty of which
is that our general framework is broadly applicable to a large class of spaces.
We approach this problem of understanding the attaching maps
from the perspective of the recently developed theory of stratified spaces of Ayala-Francis-Tanaka
[
AFT17
]
.
Stratified topological spaces are topological spaces equipped with some extra organizational structure called a stratification. By imposing some extra regularity, one arrives at the notion of a conically
smooth stratified space. Conically smooth stratified spaces encompass Euclidean spaces and manifolds, but also
capture manifolds with corners and singularities.
The extra regularity of a conically smooth stratified space allows us to observe
that the attaching maps are better than just continuous maps to a lower skeleton of a CW structure; these attaching
maps are constructible bundles. In particular, differential topology can be employed for computing the degrees of
maps, which amounts to computing determinants of matrices.
In this paper, we show how the additional structure of a
conically smooth stratification allows one to compute the homology of a space. We then apply this to the case of
real Grassmannians,
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, and provide a description of the chain complex. Further, we explicitly
determine the additive structure of the cohomology of this complex, and we provide some computations.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we define the real Grassmannian
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
as a smooth manifold. Additionally,
we describe the natural Schubert CW structure on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, and set up the problem of computing the boundary
maps of the corresponding CW chain complex. In Section
3
, we define
stratified topological spaces and conically smooth stratified spaces, and provide a number of examples.
Further, we prove that one can use a conically smooth structure to compute the homology of a space.
Section
4
introduces the Schubert stratification of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, and then uses the theory of
Section
3
to explicitly compute the boundary maps in the Schubert CW chain complex of

\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
. Using this formula for the boundary maps, in Section
5
, we establish a
closed formula for the
R
-cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
. Finally, in Section
6
, we provide
some explicit computations.

1.2
Notation

We will now fix some notational conventions that we will use throughout the paper:

•

For
S
a finite set, we let
\mathsf{card}(S)
denote the cardinality of
S
.

•

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
, we let
\underline{r}
denote the set
\{1,\dots,r\}

•

For
S
a set, let
\mathsf{Sub}(S)
denote the poset of subsets of
S
.

•

We let
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
denote the set of all cardinality
k
subsets of the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
.

•

Let
\mathsf{Mat}_{n\times k}
denote the collection of
n
-by-
k
matrices with real coefficients.

•

Let
\mathbbm{1}_{n\times n}\in\mathsf{Mat}_{n\times n}
denote the
n
-by-
n
identity matrix.

•

For each
1\leq i\leq n
,
\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the
i
-th standard basis vector of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
That is, it is the vector consisting of a 1 in the
i
-th entry, and 0’s in all other entries.

•

For
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, we define
\mathbb{R}^{S}
to be the span

\mathbb{R}^{S}:=\mathsf{span}\{\mathbf{e}_{i}~|~i\in S\}~.

•

For a
X
a topological space, we let
X^{+}
denote the set,
X\amalg\{\infty\}
, where we attach a
single disjoint point to
X
. This set is equipped with the following topology:
U\subset X^{+}
is open
iff
U
is an open subset of
X
, or
U=(X\setminus C)\amalg\{\infty\}
, for some closed and compact

C\subset X
.

•

For
\ast\in X
a pointed topological space, we let
\Sigma X
denote the reduced suspension,

\Sigma X:=(X\times[0,1])/\sim~,

where
\sim
is the equivalence relation defined by
(x,1)\sim(x^{\prime},1),(x,0)\sim(x^{\prime},0),
and

(\ast,t)\sim(\ast,t^{\prime})
, for all
x,x^{\prime}\in X
, and
t,t^{\prime}\in[0,1]
.

•

We use homological grading conventions:
A[r]_{n}:=A_{r+n}
.

2
Grassmannians

Grassmannians are the moduli space
of subspaces of a fixed vector space. In this paper, we restrict our attention
to subspaces of Euclidean space. These are the
real
Grassmannians:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
n,k
be positive integers such that
k\leq n
. We define the

Grassmannian
,
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, to be the set consisting of all
k
-dimensional subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

The simplest case to understand is when
n=1
, as
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(1)
is just a singleton.
In general,
\mathsf{Gr}_{n}(n)
is also a singleton. The first nontrivial example is
(n,k)=(2,1)
.
One way to think about this set is to observe that, by taking its span, each choice of a unit vector
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
defines a 1-dimensional subspace, hence a point in
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)
.
Further, by observing that antipodal vectors define the same subspace,
we see that the following map of sets is a bijection

[0,\pi)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2),\qquad\theta\mapsto\mathsf{span}\{(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\}~.

Thinking of a Grassmannian as a set does not capture its entire being.
In the above example of
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)\cong[0,\pi)
, if we sweep through the angles
\theta
, as we approach

\theta=\pi
, our subspace is getting closer to the
x
-axis, which is represented by the point
0\in[0,\pi)
. In
other words, thinking of
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)
as the set
[0,\pi)
, ignores the fact that points near
\pi
are actually near
0

as well. We will make this intuition precise below, and we will see that
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)
is actually diffeomorphic to

S^{1}
.

More generally, each
p\in\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
can be representated by a
k
-plane in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
and we know how to wiggle planes in Euclidean space.
This suggests two things. First, we know which planes are close to a given plane, so we might expect

\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
to be a topological space. Second, we know how to budge a plane into a nearby plane, so we might further
expect
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
to possess the structure of a manifold.
This is indeed the case, as we briefly recall in Section

2.1
. Even better than possessing a manifold structure, there is a natural way of decomposing
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)

into pieces called
Schubert cells
. These endow
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
with the structure of a CW complex. We give
a description of this Schubert CW structure and the corresponding CW chain complex in Section
2.2
.
We refer the reader to
[
MS75
]
for a more in depth treatment of this material.

2.1
Manifold structure

Let us fix positive integers
n
and
k
, with
k\leq n
. We define a manifold structure on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
by
realizing it as the quotient of an open subset of Euclidean space called the Stiefel space.

Definition 2.2
.

We define the
Stiefel space

V_{k}(n)
to be the collection of all injective, linear maps
from
\mathbb{R}^{k}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

V_{k}(n):=\{\mathbb{R}^{k}\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathbb{R}^{n}~|~\varphi\text{ is injective and linear}\}\subset\mathsf{Mat}_{n\times k}~.

A point in
V_{k}(n)
is a matrix
A\in\mathsf{Mat}_{n\times k}
whose columns are linearly independent.
Linear independence is an open condition, which means that
V_{k}(n)\subset(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\times k}
is an
open subset of Euclidean space. As such we consider
V_{k}(n)
as a topological space via the subspace topology.
Each point
A\in V_{k}(n)

defines a
k
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by taking its column space.
This defines a map

\mathsf{col}:V_{k}(n)\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n),\qquad A\mapsto\mathsf{col}(A)~.

Observe that this map is surjective: given
V\in\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, choose a basis for it and define a matrix whose columns
consist of those basis vectors. This defines a point in
V_{k}(n)
whose column space is
V
.
Thus, we endow
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
with the quotient topology induced by the map
\mathsf{col}
.
Further, one can show that
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
is a compact topological space, and that it has a natural manifold structure:

Proposition 2.3
.

The space
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
is a compact, smooth manifold of dimension
k(n-k)
.

Proof.
We defer the reader to
[
MS75
]
for the claims of compactness and Hausdorfness.
We will define a smooth atlas on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
as follows. Let
\mathsf{pr}_{S}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{S}

denote the orthogonal projection
x\mapsto\sum_{i\in S}\langle x,e_{i}\rangle e_{i}
.
Given
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, consider the subset

U_{S}:=\left\{V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}~|~V\xhookrightarrow{\iota}\mathbb{R}^{n}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{pr}_{S}}\mathbb{R}^{S}\text{ is an isomorphism}\right\}\subset\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)~.

Define a map

\text{Hom}^{\text{lin}}(\mathbb{R}^{S},\mathbb{R}^{\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus S})\to U_{S}

whose value on an injective linear map
F
is its graph:

F\mapsto\mathsf{Graph}(F):=\{x+F(x)~|~x\in\mathbb{R}^{S}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}~.

In fact, this map is a bijection with inverse given by the following:
given
V\in U_{S}
, define a linear map
F\in\text{Hom}^{\text{lin}}(\mathbb{R}^{S},\mathbb{R}^{\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus S})

via the assignment

e_{i}\mapsto\mathsf{pr}_{S}^{-1}(e_{i})\cap V-e_{i}~,

for all
i\in S
.
Further, one can check that this in fact defines a homeomorphism.
Thus, for each
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
,
we have a homeomorphism

\varphi_{S}:\mathbb{R}^{k(n-k)}\cong\text{Hom}^{\text{lin}}(\mathbb{R}^{S},\mathbb{R}^{\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus S})\xrightarrow{\cong}U_{S}~.

We leave it as an exercise to check that the collection
\{\mathbb{R}^{k(n-k)}\xrightarrow{\varphi_{S}}U_{S}\}

defines a smooth atlas for
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
. (See e.g. 7.8 in
[
Tu08
]
.)
∎

2.2
CW structure

There is a natural CW structure on Grassmannians: the
Schubert
CW structure. The set of Schubert cells
is in bijection with the set
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
:

Definition 2.4
.

For positive integers
k\leq n
, let

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}:=\{S\subset\{1,\dots,n\}~|~\mathsf{card}(S)=k\}~,

denote the set consisting of all cardinality
k
subsets of the set
\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Define a partial order on the elements of
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
by declaring

S=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}\leq T=\{t_{1}<\cdots<t_{k}\}
to mean
s_{i}\leq t_{i}
for each
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.

Example 2.5
.

The poset
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{4}{2}
can be depicted as

For
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, the Schubert cell corresponding to
S

is the subspace

\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}:=\{V\in\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)~|~\text{$S$ is the maximal element in }{\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}}\text{ for which }V\in U_{S}\}~.

One can check that such a maximum indeed exists, and is unique.
For example, consider
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{2}{1}=\{\{1\}<\{2\}\}
, and take
S=\{1\}
. Then
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)_{\{1\}}

is simply a singleton, because the only 1-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that does not project isomorphically
onto the
y
-axis,
\mathbb{R}^{\{2\}}=\mathsf{span}\{e_{2}\}
, is the
x
-axis,
\mathbb{R}^{\{1\}}=\mathsf{span}\{e_{1}\}
. This also
tells us that
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)_{\{2\}}
consists of all other points in
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)
. Thus,
\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)
is comprised of a single
0-dimensional cell,

\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)_{\{1\}}=\left\{\mathsf{span}\begin{bmatrix}1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\right\}~,

and a single 1-dimensional cell,

\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(2)_{\{2\}}=\left\{\mathsf{span}\begin{bmatrix}\ast\\
1\end{bmatrix}~\bigg|~\ast\in\mathbb{R}\right\}~,

as depicted by the
following picture

\hbox to138.86pt{\vbox to115.41pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 57.70552pt\lower-57.70552pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

To name a CW structure, one must define attaching maps that specify how the higher dimensional cells
attach to the lower dimensional cells.
We will now explicitly describe this cellular decomposition of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
.

For each
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, we define
a map
\mathsf{Rot}_{S}
from a closed cube to
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, whose interior is homeomorphic with
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}
, and whose
boundary maps to strictly lower dimensional cells.
To define
\mathsf{Rot}_{S}
, we will use a product of rotation matrices, which are elements of
O(n)
. Since
O(n)
is
non-Abelian, the order in which we multiply these matrices is critical. To obtain the correct order of multiplication,
we use a particularly ordered index set,
Z_{S}
:

Definition 2.6
.

For
S=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
,
define the set

Z_{S}:=\left\{(i,j)\mid i\in\{1,\dots,k\},j\in\{i,\dots,s_{i}-1\}\right\}~.

We equip this set with a total ordering by declaring
(i,j)\leq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})
to mean either
i<i^{\prime}
, or
i=i^{\prime}
and

j\geq j^{\prime}
. See Remark
2.7
for a further explanation of this ordering.

For
1\leq j<n
and
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\mathsf{R}_{j}(\theta)
denote the
n\times n
matrix implementing
a rotation of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
in the oriented
(j<j+1)
-plane by an angle of
\theta
:

\mathsf{R}_{j}(\theta):=\begin{bmatrix}&&&&&&&&\\
&\mathbbm{1}_{j-1\times j-1}&&&&&&&\\
&&&&&&&&\\
&&&\cos(\theta)&-\sin(\theta)&&&&\\
&&&\sin(\theta)&\cos(\theta)&&&&\\
&&&&&&&&\\
&&&&&&&\mathbbm{1}_{(n-j-1)\times(n-j-1)}&\\
&&&&&&&&\end{bmatrix}~,

where the middle block lies in the
j
and
j+1
rows and columns, and all unspecified entries are 0.
For example, for
n=3
,

\mathsf{R}_{2}(\theta)=\begin{bmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\\
0&\sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{bmatrix}~.

Note that for each
j
, this defines a continuous map

[0,\pi]\to O(n),\qquad\theta\mapsto\mathsf{R}_{j}(\theta)~.

Given a matrix
A\in O(n)
, the first
k
columns of
A
is an ordered set of
k
linearly independent vectors in

\mathbb{R}^{n}
, which is a point in
V_{k}(n)
.
Note the map

O(n)\to V_{k}(n),\qquad A\mapsto A\mathbbm{1}_{n\times k}~,

where
\mathbbm{1}_{n\times k}
denotes the first
k
columns of the
n\times n
identity matrix. This map is
evidently continuous, since matrix multiplication is continuous.

Remark 2.7
.

The reason for the above defined ordering on
Z_{S}
is to ensure that the matrix multiplication in the
definition of
\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}

occurs in the correct sequence. Namely, we have set this up so that if we evaluate
\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
at the point

(\pi/2)_{Z_{S}}
, then we obtain the plane
\mathbb{R}^{S}
. The defined ordering first moves
\mathbf{e}_{k}
into
\mathbf{e}_{s_{k}}
,
and ensures that the subsequent rotations will not move

\mathbf{e}_{s_{k}}
further. Next, it moves
\mathbf{e}_{k-1}
into
\mathbf{e}_{s_{k-1}}
, again ensuring that subsequent rotations will
not move either
\mathbf{e}_{s_{k}}
or
\mathbf{e}_{s_{k-1}}
. This process continues, until the final result produces
\mathbb{R}^{S}
.

These maps, together with the group structure of
O(n)
, and the particularly ordered index set,
Z_{S}

(as just explained in Remark
2.7
),
allow us to define the composite continuous map

\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}:\prod_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}[0,\pi]\xrightarrow{(\mathsf{R}_{j}(-))_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}}\prod_{Z_{S}}O(n)\xrightarrow{\text{mult}}O(n)\xrightarrow{-\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{n\times k}}V_{k}(n)~,

that sends

(\theta_{(i,j)})_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\mapsto\left(\prod_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\mathsf{R}_{j}(\theta_{(i,j)})\right)\mathbbm{1}_{n\times k}~.

Finally, postcomposing with
\mathsf{col}:V_{k}(n)\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, we obtain the continuous map

\mathsf{Rot}_{S}:\prod_{Z_{S}}[0,\pi]\xrightarrow{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}}V_{k}(n)\xrightarrow{\mathsf{col}}\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)~.

Having defined the attaching maps, we now show that the collection of sets
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}
do form a CW structure
on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
.
First,
we establsih a homeomorphism of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}
with the interior of the closed disk
\prod_{Z_{S}}[0,\pi]
.
In particular, this will tell us that the dimension of the cell
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}
is the cardinality of
Z_{S}
. Let us
denote this dimension by
d(S)
:

d(S):=\mathsf{card}(Z_{S})=\mathsf{dim}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S})~.

Observation 2.8
.

Recall that for
S=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
,

d(S):=\mathsf{card}(Z_{S})=\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,k\}}\mathsf{card}(\{i,\dots,s_{i}-1\})=\sum_{i\in\{1,\dots,k\}}s_{i}-i~.

Lemma 2.9
.

For
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, the restriction of
\mathsf{Rot}_{S}
to the interior of
\prod_{Z_{S}}[0,\pi]
,

\mathsf{Rot}_{S}:\prod_{Z_{S}}(0,\pi)\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)~,

is a homeomorphism onto
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}
.

Proof.
We prove this by induction on
k
.
First, assume
k=1
, and let
S=\{s\}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{1}
. Note that for
V\in\mathsf{Gr}_{1}(n)_{S}
,

\mathsf{col}^{-1}(V)=\left\{\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{s},0,\dots,0)~|~\sum x_{i}^{2}=1,~x_{s}\neq 0,~\mathsf{col}(\mathbf{x})\cong V\right\}~.

Observe there is a unique
\mathbf{x}\in\mathsf{col}^{-1}(V)
such that
x_{s}>0
. Further, we claim there is a unique element

\theta^{\mathbf{x}}\in\prod_{Z_{S}}(0,\pi)
such that
\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\theta^{\mathbf{x}})=\mathbf{x}
. As we now explain, this follows from the
injectivity of each
\mathsf{R}_{j}:(0,\pi)\to O(n)
, and the order in which the
\mathsf{R}_{j}
matrices are multiplied, i.e., the order
of
Z_{S}
. Note that
Z_{S}=\{(1,s-1)<\cdots<(1,1)\}
. As explained in Remark
2.7
, this ordering on

Z_{S}
is such that the standard basis vector
\mathbf{e}_{1}
gets rotated into
\mathbf{x}
by first rotating in the
(1,2)
-plane
by an angle
\theta_{(1,1)}\in(0,\pi)
, and then rotating in the
(2,3)
-plane by an angle
\theta_{(1,2)}\in(0,\pi)
,
etc., until a final rotation in the
(s-1,s)
-plane by an angle
\theta_{(1,s-1)}\in(0,\pi)
. Since a rotation in
the
(j,j+1)
-plane does not effect the
j-1
-coordinate, we see that for each
j\in\{1,\dots,s-1\}
, there is a
unique value
\theta^{\mathbf{x}}_{(1,j)}\in(0,\pi)
such that the first
j
coordinates of the vector

\mathsf{R}_{j}(\theta^{\mathbf{x}}_{(1,j)})\mathsf{R}_{j-1}(\theta^{\mathbf{x}}_{(1,j-1)})\cdots\mathsf{R}_{1}(\theta^{\mathbf{x}}_{(1,1)})\mathbf{e}_{1}

are equal to the first
j
coordinates of
\mathbf{x}
. This completes the base case.

Now, assume
k>1
. For
S=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k+1}\}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k+1}
, let
\widehat{S}
denote the set

S\setminus\{s_{k+1}\}
. Note that for
V\in\mathsf{Gr}_{k+1}(n)_{S}
,

\mathsf{col}^{-1}(V)=\left\{\left.A=\begin{bmatrix}a_{1,1}&\cdots&a_{1,\ell}&\cdots&a_{1,k+1}\\
\vdots&&&&\\
a_{s_{1},1}&&\vdots&&\\
0&&&&\vdots\\
&&a_{s_{\ell},\ell}&&\\
&&0&&\\
\vdots&&&&a_{s_{k+1},k+1}\\
&&\vdots&&0\\
&&&&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&0\\
\end{bmatrix}~\right|~\mathbf{a}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{a}_{j}=\delta_{i}^{j},~\mathsf{col}(A)\cong V,~a_{s_{i},i}\neq 0\right\}~,

where
\mathbf{a}_{i}
denotes the
i
th column of
A
, and
\delta_{i}^{j}
is the Kronecker delta. Again, there is a
unique
A_{V}\in\mathsf{col}^{-1}(V)
such that
a_{s_{i},i}>0
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k+1\}
.
From the induction hypothesis, we know the map

\mathsf{Rot}_{\widehat{S}}:\prod_{Z_{\widehat{S}}}(0,\pi)\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{\widehat{S}}

is a homeomorphism. By an analysis similar to that of the base case, we can extend this to a homeomorphism

\mathsf{Rot}_{S}:\prod_{Z_{S}}(0,\pi)\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k+1}(n)_{S}

by including the extra necessary rotations to move
\mathbf{e}_{k+1}
to the last column vector of
A_{V}
.
In particular,
Z_{S}\setminus Z_{\widehat{S}}=\{(k+1,s_{k+1}-1),\dots,(k+1,k+1)\}
, so we define

\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}:=\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{\widehat{S}}\circ\mathsf{R}_{s_{k+1}}(\theta_{(k+1,s_{k+1})})\cdots\mathsf{R}_{k+1}(\theta_{(k+1,k+1)})~,

for some choice of angles. As before, there is a unique choice of angles in
(0,\pi)
to achieve these rotations, and
the addition of these rotations will only effect
\mathbf{e}_{k+1}
.
∎

Next, we establish that the boundaries of these cells attach to strictly lower dimensional cells. In what follows,
denote

\partial\prod_{Z_{S}}[0,\pi]:=\prod_{Z_{S}}[0,\pi]\setminus\prod_{Z_{S}}(0,\pi)~.

Lemma 2.10
.

For
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, the restriction of
\mathsf{Rot}_{S}
to the boundary factors through the union of the lower
dimensional cells:

\hbox to229.56pt{\vbox to80.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 114.77927pt\lower-39.57051pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-114.77927pt}{-20.59761pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{{
{
\lxSVG@setdash{0.0pt}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@miterjoin\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@roundcap\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

}
\lxSVG@stroke}}

\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope {

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~.

Proof.
Let
\Theta=(\theta_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}\in\partial\displaystyle\prod_{Z_{S}}[0,\pi]
.
We will show that for each
1\leq i\leq k
, and each
j>s_{i}
, the
(j,i)
entry of
\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta)
is 0. Fix such a

(j,i)
pair. Let

B:=\prod\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in Z_{S},~i^{\prime}\leq i}R_{j^{\prime}}(\theta_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})})~,

and

A:=\prod\limits_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in Z_{S},~i^{\prime}>i}R_{j^{\prime}}(\theta_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})})~.

Since
j\geq s_{i}+1
, and each
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in Z_{S}
with
i^{\prime}\leq i
has
j^{\prime}<s_{i}
, the
j
-th row of
B
is
\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}
. Further,
since each
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in Z_{S}
with
i^{\prime}>i
has
j^{\prime}\geq i^{\prime}>i
, the
i
-th column of
A
is
\mathbf{e}_{i}
. Finally, since

i\leq s_{i}<j
,
\mathbf{e}_{j}^{T}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}=0
.
∎

Proposition 2.11
.

The collection

\left\{(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S},\mathsf{Rot}_{S})~|~S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\right\}

defines a CW structure on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
. ∎

Proof.
This follows immediately from Lemmas
2.9
and
2.10
.
∎

2.3
The Schubert CW chain complex

To give a general description of the CW chain complex for the Schubert CW structure on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
,
we need to know how many cells there are of each
dimension. It turns out that these can be counted by a partition function. For positive integers
k,n
with
k<n
,
and
1\leq m\leq k(n-k)
, define

p_{k}^{n}(m):=\left\{\{i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\}~|~\sum_{1\leq j\leq\ell}i_{j}=m,~\ell\leq k,~i_{j}\leq n-k\right\}~.

to be the set of all partitions of
m
as the sum of at most
k
positive
integers, each of which is less than or equal to
n-k
. For consistency, let us define
p_{k}^{n}(0):=\{0\}
.

Lemma 2.12
.

There is a bijection between the set of
m
-dimensional cells of the Schubert decomposition of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)

and the set
p_{k}^{n}(m)
.

Proof.
Consider the map of sets

p_{k}^{n}(m)\xrightarrow{\varphi}\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}~,

via the assignment

\{i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{\ell}\}\mapsto\{1,2,\dots,k-\ell,k-(\ell-1)+i_{1},k-(\ell-2)+i_{2},\dots,k+i_{\ell}\}~.

First note that
\{1,2,\dots,k-\ell,k-(\ell-1)+i_{1},k-(\ell-2)+i_{2},\dots,k+i_{\ell}\}
indeed has cardinality
k
, and is thus
an element of
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
. By definition, this map is injective. We will now show that the image of
\varphi

is the collection of sets
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
such that
d(S)=m
.
Let
S=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}
be such that
d(S)=m
. By Observation
2.8
,

\sum_{j=1}^{k}s_{j}-j=m~.

Therefore there exists some set
L\subset\{1,\dots,k\}
where for each
j\in L
,
s_{j}-j\neq 0
and

\sum_{j\in L}s_{j}-j=m~.

Then
I:=\{s_{j}-j~|~j\in L\}
is an element of
p^{n}_{k}(m)
for which
\varphi(I)=S
.
Finally, we show that the only sets in the image of
\varphi
are those
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
for which
d(S)=m
.
Given
S=\{1,2,\dots,k-\ell,k-(\ell-1)+i_{1},\dots,k+i_{\ell}\}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
in the image of

\varphi
, by Observation
2.8
, the dimension of the corresponding Schubert cell is

d(S)=\sum_{j=1}^{k}s_{j}-j=i_{1}+\cdots+i_{\ell}=m~.

∎

Lemma
2.12

tells us that the CW chain complex for the Schubert CW structure on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
is of the form

0\to\bigoplus_{p^{n}_{k}(k(n-k))}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\partial_{k(n-k)}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\partial_{j+1}}\bigoplus_{p^{n}_{k}(j)}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\partial_{j}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\partial_{1}}\bigoplus_{p^{n}_{k}(0)}\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\partial_{0}}0~.

Therefore, in order to compute the homology of this chain complex, we need to have a description of these
boundary maps. Proposition
3.17
, to come, will provide us with a way of computing these
boundary maps.

3
Homology of stratified spaces

As we saw in the preceeding section, the remaining task to compute the homology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
is to
determine the differentials. In order to do this, we exploit some natural extra regularity on the Schubert
decomposition of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, namely,
a stratification. For the purposes of computing homology, we need to impose some further regularity on this stratification, that of a
conically smooth structure
. In this section we give a brief introduction to the theory of stratified spaces
à la
[
AFT17
]
, including the notion of conical smoothness, and then we
discuss how this theory enables us to compute the homology of conically smooth stratified spaces.

3.1
Stratified spaces

In this section, we introduce the basic definitions of the theory of stratified spaces that we will use in the
remainder of the paper.

Definition 3.1
.

A
stratified topologcial space
is a triple
(X\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathcal{P})
consisting of

•

a paracompact, Hausdorff topological space,
X
,

•

a poset
\mathcal{P}
, and

•

a continuous map
\psi:X\to\mathcal{P}
, where
\mathcal{P}
is equipped with the downward closed topology:

U\subset\mathcal{P}
is closed if for each
p\in U
, if
q<p
, then
q\in U
.

Definition 3.2
.

For
X\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{P}
a stratified topological space, and
p\in\mathcal{P}
, the
p
-stratum

of
X
is the subspace

X_{p}:=\varphi^{-1}(p)\subset X~.

Example 3.3
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold, and let
W\hookrightarrow M
be a properly embedded submanifold. The map

M\to\{0<1\},\qquad m\mapsto\begin{cases}0,&\quad\text{if }m\in W\\
1,&\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

exhibits
M
as a stratified topological space with strata
M_{0}=W
and
M_{1}=M\setminus W
.

Example 3.4
.

Let
X
be a topological space equipped with a CW structure.
The skeleta of the CW structure give rise to a stratification of
X
.
The stratifying poset is the nonnegative integers with their natural partial order,
(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0},\leq)
.
For
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, let
X_{k}
denote the
k
-skeleton of
X
. That is,
X_{k}
is the union of all cells,

X_{\alpha}
, in the CW structure of
X
such that
\mathsf{dim}(X_{\alpha})\leq k
:

X_{k}:=\bigcup_{\mathsf{dim}(X_{\alpha})\leq k}X_{\alpha}~.

Notice that each element
x\in X
lies in
X_{k}\setminus X_{k-1}
for some unique
k
.
Define the map
X\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
to be such that
x\in X
maps to the unique
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that

x\in X_{k}\setminus X_{k-1}.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{P}
be a minimal element of a poset. The
link of
\mathcal{P}
along
p
is defined to be the
poset

\mathsf{Link}_{p}(\mathcal{P}):=\mathcal{P}_{p<}:=\{q\in\mathcal{P}~|~p<q\}~.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
p\in\mathcal{P}
be a minimal element of a poset. The
blowup of
\mathcal{P}
along
p
is defined to be
the poset

\mathsf{Bl}_{p}(\mathcal{P}):=\mathsf{Link}_{p}(\mathcal{P})\times\{0<1\}\coprod_{\mathsf{Link}_{p}(\mathcal{P})\times\{1\}}\mathcal{P}\setminus\{p\}~.

.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
X\to\mathcal{P}
be a smooth manifold equipped with the additional structure of a stratified topological
space. Further, assume that each stratum,
X_{p}
, is a smooth submanifold of
X
. For
p\in\mathcal{P}
a minimal element of the stratifying poset, we define the
link of
X
along
X_{p}
to be the
stratified topological space with underlying space the unit sphere bundle of the normal bundle

\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X):=S(N_{X_{p}\subset X})~,

and stratifying poset given by
\mathsf{Link}_{p}(\mathcal{P})
.

Note that this definition in fact exhibits the link as a smooth fiber bundle

\pi:\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)\to X_{p}~.

A choice of a tubular neighborhood of
X_{p}\subset X
gives us a smooth map

N_{X_{p}\subset X}\hookrightarrow X
.
We then get a smooth map from the thickened link to
X
:

\gamma:\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)\times(0,\infty)\hookrightarrow N_{X_{p}\subset X}\hookrightarrow X~.

Definition 3.9
.

Let
X\to\mathcal{P}
be a smooth manifold equipped with the additional structure of a stratified topological space, and let
p\in\mathcal{P}
be a minimal element.
We define the
blowup of
X
along
X_{p}
to be the pushout

\mathsf{Bl}_{X_{p}}(X):=\left(\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)\times[0,\infty)\right)\coprod_{\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)\times(0,\infty)}X\setminus X_{p}~.

This space is naturally stratified by the poset
\mathsf{Bl}_{p}(\mathcal{P})
.

Example 3.10
.

We can stratify
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by the poset
\mathcal{P}
from Example
3.7
. We specify the structure map

\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{P}
in Figure
1
by labeling the strata. There is a single 0-dimensional
stratum
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{O}
consisting of the origin.
The resulting blowup and link of
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{P})
along
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{O}
are given in Figure
1
.

NE
NW
SE
SW
N
S
E
W
O

Figure 1:
Left:
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{P})
with indicated strata. Middle: the blowup
\mathsf{Bl}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{O}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{P})
. Right: the link
\mathsf{Link}_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{O}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathcal{P}).

Example 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a poset. We define the set of
subdivisions
of
\mathcal{P}
to consist of all
subsets
S\subset\mathcal{P}
such that
S
is nonempty, finite, and the partial order on
S
induced by
the partial order of
\mathcal{P}
is a linear order. There is a natural partial order of the subdivisions of a poset
given by inclusion of sets. We denote this poset by
\mathsf{sd}(\mathcal{P})
.
The closed interval
[0,1]
admits a natural stratification by the poset,
\mathsf{sd}(\{0<1\})
.
The map
[0,1]\to\mathsf{sd}(\{0<1\})
is given by

x\mapsto\begin{cases}\{0\},&\text{ if }x=0\\
\{1\},&\text{ if }x=1\\
\{0,1\},&\text{ else}\end{cases}~.

More generally, let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. The following topological space

\mathsf{Cube}^{k}:=[0,1]^{\times k}~,

admits a natural stratification by
\mathsf{sd}\left(\{0<1\}^{\times k}\right)
. This can be seen by taking the

k
-fold product of the above stratified space,
[0,1]\to\mathsf{sd}(\{0<1\})
, and then invoking the fact
that
\left(\mathsf{sd}(\{0<1\})\right)^{\times k}\cong\mathsf{sd}\left(\{0<1\}^{\times k}\right)
.

3.2
Conical smoothness

By imposing some extra regularity on a
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-stratified manifold, we can use the stratification to compute the
homology of the manifold. This is the notion of a
conically smooth structure
, which is analogous to a
smooth structure on a topological space.
We begin by briefly narrating key features of smooth structures on topological spaces (see
[
Tu08
]
for an introduction).

Among paracompact Hausdorff topological spaces,
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-manifolds are characterized by being locally Euclidean, that is, each point has a neighborhood that is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{i}
for some
i\geq 0
.
A
smooth structure
is a type of
regularity
on a
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-manifold.
A
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}
-manifold, or
smooth
manifold, is a
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-manifold equipped with a smooth structure.
There is a distinguished class of continuous maps between two smooth manifolds, called the
smooth
maps. This class of smooth maps consists precisely of those continuous maps that respect this smooth structure.
Here, “respect” is just so that points (2) and (3) are true.
This regularity of a smooth manifold
M
is tailored precisely so that it has the following features:

1.

For each point
x\in M
, there is a
tangent space
,
T_{x}M
, which is a vector space.
This tangent space is a canonical local model of
M
about
x
, which is to say there is a basis for the topology about
x\in M
comprised of images of smooth open embeddings
\varphi_{x}:T_{x}M\hookrightarrow M
each that carries
0
to
x

2.

For
f:M\to N
a smooth map, and for each
x\in M
there is a linear map,

D_{x}f:T_{x}M\longrightarrow T_{f(x)}N~,

called the
derivative
of
f
at
x
.
Through a choice of smooth open embeddings
T_{x}M\xhookrightarrow{\varphi_{x}}M
and
T_{f(x)}N\xhookrightarrow{\psi_{f(x)}}N
as in the above point for which
f({\sf Image}(\varphi_{x}))\subset{\sf Image}(\psi_{f(x)})
, this derivative can be identified as the limit

T_{x}M\xrightarrow{\varphi_{x}}{\sf Image}(\varphi_{x})\xrightarrow{~f~}{\sf Image}(\psi_{f(x)})T_{f(x)}N~,\qquad v\mapsto\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\psi_{f(x)}^{-1}f\varphi_{x}(tv)}{t}~.

(2)

3.

The map (
2
) depends smoothly on
x\in M
, appropriately interpreted.

Example 3.12
.

Let
f=(f^{1},\dots,f^{n})\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a sequence of
n
polynomial maps, each in
m
variables.
Suppose, for each
x\in f^{-1}(0)
, that the total Jacobian matrix of
f
at
x
,

\Bigl[{\partial_{i}f^{j}}_{|x}\Bigr]~,

has rank
n
.
The
Regular Value Theorem
states that the subspace
f^{-1}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
has the natural structure of a smooth manifold.

We now give a similar brief narrative of features of conically smooth structures on stratified spaces (see
[
AFT17
]
for an account).
Among paracompact Hausdorff stratified topological spaces,
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-stratified spaces are characterized by being locally a product of a Euclidean space and an open cone:
\mathbb{R}^{i}\times\mathsf{C}(L)
for some
i\geq 0
and compact
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-stratified space.
While this seems circular, because the topological dimension of
\mathsf{C}(L)
is strictly greater than that of
L
, this notion can be grounded through induction (on dimension).
A
conically smooth
structure is a type of
regularity
on a
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-stratified space.
A
\mathsf{C}^{\infty}
-stratified space, or
conically smooth
stratified space, is a
\mathsf{C}^{0}
-stratified space equipped with a conically smooth structure.
There is a special class of continuous maps between two conically smooth stratified spaces, called the
conically smooth
maps. This class of maps consists precisely of those continuous maps which respect this conically smooth structure.
Here,“respect” is just so that points (2) and (3) are true.
This regularity of a conically smooth stratified space
X
is tailored precisely so that it has the following features:

1.

For each point
x\in X
, there is a
tangent cone
,
T_{x}X\times\mathsf{C}(L_{x})
, which is a product of a vector space and an open cone – such a product has a scaling action by the group
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
.
This tangent cone is a canonical local model of
X
about
x
, which is to say there is a basis for the topology about
x\in X
comprised of images of smooth open embeddings
\varphi_{x}\colon T_{x}X\times\mathsf{C}(L_{x})\hookrightarrow X
each that carries
0
to
x
.

2.

For
f\colon X\to Y
a conically smooth map, and for each
x\in X
there is a
\mathbb{R}^{\times}
-equivariant map,

D_{x}f\colon T_{x}X\times\mathsf{C}(L_{x})\longrightarrow T_{f(x)}Y\times\mathsf{C}(L_{f(x)})~,

called the
derivative
of
f
at
x
.
Through a choice of smooth open embeddings
T_{x}X\times\mathsf{C}(L_{x})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{x}}X
and
T_{f(x)}Y\times\mathsf{C}(L_{f(x)})\xrightarrow{\psi_{f(x)}}Y
as in the above point for which
f({\sf Image}(\varphi_{x}))\subset{\sf Image}(\psi_{f(x)})
, this derivative can be identified as the limit

T_{x}X\times\mathsf{C}(L_{x})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{x}}{\sf Image}(\varphi_{x})\xrightarrow{~f~}{\sf Image}(\psi_{f(x)})\xrightarrow{\psi_{f(x)}^{-1}}T_{f(x)}Y\times\mathsf{C}(L_{f(x)})~,

(3)

(v,[s,l])~\mapsto~\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{\psi_{f(x)}^{-1}f\varphi_{x}(tv,[ts,l])}{t}~.

3.

The map (
3
) depends conically smoothly on
x\in X
, appropriately interpreted.

Example 3.13
.

Let
f=(f^{1},\dots,f^{n})\colon\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a sequence of
n
polynomial maps each in
m
variables.
The Thom-Mather Theorem
[
Mat12
]
states that, with no assumptions on the total Jacobian matrix, the subspace
f^{-1}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
has the natural structure of a conically smooth stratified space.

Example 3.14
.

Let
W\subset M
be a properly embedded smooth
d
-submanifold of a smooth
n
-manifold.
Assume
d<n
.
Consider the stratified topological space

(W\subset M):=\Bigl(M\xrightarrow{x\mapsto d~\text{iff}~x\in W}\{d<n\}\Bigr)~.

So the
d
-stratum is
W=(W\subset M)_{d}
, and the
n
-stratum is
M\setminus W=(W\subset M)_{n}
.
For each
x\in W
, consider the vector space
N_{x}:=\frac{T_{x}M}{T_{x}W}
.
Consider the unit sphere of this vector space
L_{x}:=S(N_{x}):=(N_{x}\setminus 0)_{/\mathbb{R}_{>0}}
– it is diffeomorphic with a
(n-d-1)
-sphere, and its open cone
\mathsf{C}(L_{x})\cong\mathbb{R}^{n-d}
is homeomorphic with
(n-d)
-Euclidean space.
A choice of tubular neighborhood of
W\subset M
determines, for each
x\in W
, an open embedding
\varphi_{x}\colon T_{x}W\times\mathsf{C}(L_{x})\hookrightarrow M
that carries
0
to
x
.
These open embeddings determine a conically smooth structure on the stratified topological space
(W\subset M)
.

The following is a catalogue of the key features of conically smooth stratified space
X=(X\to P)
:

1.

For each
p\in P
, the stratum
X_{p}
is equipped with the structure of a connected smooth manifold.

2.

For each strictly related pair
p<q
in
P
, there is a smooth manifold
{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}
with boundary
\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}
as well as a proper quotient map

\overline{\pi}_{p<q}\colon{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\longrightarrow X_{p}\cup X_{q}

to the union in
X
of the
p
- and the
q
-strata.
This continuous map
\overline{\pi}_{p<q}
has the following features.

(a)

The preimage of the
p
-stratum is precisely the boundary of
{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}
; equivalently, the preimage of the
q
-stratum is precisely the interior of
{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}
:

\overline{\pi}_{p<q}^{-1}(X_{p})~=~\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\overline{\pi}_{p<q}^{-1}(X_{q})~=~{\sf Interior}\bigl({\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\bigr)~.

(b)

The restriction of
\overline{\pi}_{p<q}
to the interior of
{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}
is a diffeomorphism onto the
q
-stratum:

(\overline{\pi}_{p<q})_{|}\colon{\sf Interior}\bigl({\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\bigr)\xrightarrow{~\cong~}X_{q}~.

(c)

The restriction of
\overline{\pi}_{p<q}
to the boundary of
{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}
is a smooth fiber bundle:

\pi_{p<q}\colon\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\longrightarrow X_{q}~.

Points (a)-(c) can be summarized as a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sf Interior}\bigl({\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\bigr)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{p}\cup X_{q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{p<q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm inclusion}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\overline{\pi}}_{p<q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm inclusion}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm inclusion}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm inclusion}

in which each square is both a pullback and the left square is a pushout.
In particular, a choice of collaring of the boundary,

\overline{\gamma}_{p<q}\colon\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\times[0,1)\hookrightarrow{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}~,

restricts as a smooth open embedding

\gamma_{p<q}\colon\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}\times(0,1)\hookrightarrow X_{q}~.

(4)

3.

For strictly related triples
p<q<r
in
P
, there is a smooth manifold with
\langle 2\rangle
-corners
{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}\cup X_{q}}(X)_{r}
, together with a proper quotient map
\overline{\pi}_{p<q<r}\colon{\sf Bl}_{X_{p}\cup X_{q}}(X)_{r}\to X_{p}\cup X_{q}\cup X_{r}
with similar features to (a)-(c) above.

4.

Etcetera, for finite strictly monotonic sequences
p_{1}<\dots<p_{\ell}
in
P
.

Example 3.15
.

We follow up on Example
3.14
.
Namely, the smooth structures on the strata
W=(W\subset M)_{d}
and on
M\setminus W=(W\subset M)_{n}
are the given ones inherited from the given smooth structure on
M
.
The link

\Bigl(\mathsf{Link}_{W}\bigl((W\subset M)\bigr)\xrightarrow{~\pi_{d<n}~}W\Bigr)~=~\Bigl(S^{\sf fib}(N_{W\subset M})\xrightarrow{~\mathsf{pr}~}W\Bigr)

is identical with the unit sphere bundle of the normal bundle of
W\subset M
, which is a smooth manifold.
The smooth
n
-manifold with boundary
{\sf Bl}_{W}\bigl((W\subset M)\bigr)
is the
real
blow-up of
M
along
W
.
The interior of this real blow-up is the complement
M\setminus W
.
See Figure
2
in the case that
(W\subset M)=(\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Figure 2:
Left: the stratified space
(\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Middle: the blowup
\mathsf{Bl}_{\{0\}}\bigl((\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)
. Right: the link
\mathsf{Link}_{\{0\}}\bigl((\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2})\bigr)
.

Notation 3.16
.

Let
X\to\mathcal{P}
be a conically smooth stratified space, and let
p<q
be a pair of strictly related elements of

\mathcal{P}
. We will often denote the link by

\mathsf{L}_{p<q}(X):=\mathsf{Link}_{X_{p}}(X)_{q}~.

3.3
Links and homology

We now explain how the previously defined links allow one to compute the homology of a conically smooth
stratified space.
In this section, we restrict attention to topological spaces,
X
, that possess the following structure:

•

X
is a smooth manifold;

•

X
is equipped with a conically smooth structure
X\to\mathcal{P}
that is compatible with the smooth
structure;

•

Each stratum,
X_{p}
, is diffeomorphic to Euclidean space, and we further fix such a diffeomorphism

\alpha_{p}:X_{p}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{dim}(X_{p})}~,

for each
p\in\mathcal{P}
.

This structure gives rise to a well-defined map of posets

\mathsf{d}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}~,\qquad p\mapsto\mathsf{dim}(X_{p})~.

We let
X_{(i)}
denote the fiber of the composite
X\to\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
:

\hbox to96.72pt{\vbox to85.48pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 48.35904pt\lower-42.73946pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Fixing diffeomorphisms,
\alpha_{p}
for each
p\in\mathcal{P}
, endows each
stratum with an orientation, and thus an isomorphism
C^{\text{red}}_{*}(X_{p}^{+})\cong_{\alpha_{p}}\mathbb{Z}[\mathsf{dim}(X_{p})]
between the reduced
chain complex of
X_{p}^{+}
and
\mathbb{Z}
in dimension
\mathsf{dim}(X_{p})
. So to each such
conically smooth stratified space, we obtain a
sequence of abelian groups

\hbox to438.7pt{\vbox to18.38pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 219.34534pt\lower-9.1875pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-219.34534pt}{-1.97223pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~,

where
\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\rangle
sits in degree
i
.

To name
a homomorphism

\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i+1)})\rangle\xrightarrow{\partial_{i+1}}\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\rangle

is to specify a
\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\times\pi_{0}(X_{(i+1)})
-matrix. Each element of

\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})
is a stratum,
X_{p}
, of
X
with
\mathsf{dim}(X_{p})=i
.
First, for each
p<q
in
\mathcal{P}
, such that
\mathsf{dim}(X_{p})+1=\mathsf{dim}(X_{q})
, we define a map

\sigma_{p}^{q}:\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{p<q}(X))\to\{\pm 1\}

by

[\ell]\mapsto\mathsf{det}\left(\mathbb{R}^{i}\oplus\mathbb{R}\cong T_{\pi(\ell)}X_{p}\oplus\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{(D_{\ell}\pi)^{-1}\oplus\text{id}}T_{\ell}\mathsf{L}_{p<q}(X)\oplus\mathbb{R}\cong T_{(\ell,\varepsilon/2)}(\mathsf{L}_{p<q}(X)\times(0,\varepsilon))\xrightarrow{D_{\ell}\gamma}T_{\gamma(\ell)}X_{q}\cong\mathbb{R}^{i+1}\right)~.

Now, we define the
(p,q)
entry of

\partial_{i+1}:\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i+1)})\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\rangle
to be

\sum_{[\ell]\in\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{p<q}(X))}\sigma_{p}^{q}([\ell])~.

(5)

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
X\to\mathcal{P}
be a compact, connected, conically smooth stratified manifold
such that each stratum is diffeomorphic to a Euclidean space. The previously defined sequence of abelian
groups, and homomorphisms between them

\partial_{i+1}:\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i+1)})\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\rangle~,

is a chain complex. Furthermore, the homology of this chain complex is isomorphic with

H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
, the singular homology of
X
with
\mathbb{Z}
coefficients.

Proof.
The filtration

X_{(\leq 0)}\hookrightarrow X_{(\leq 1)}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow X_{(\leq\ell)}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow X~,

of
X
induces a filtration of
C_{*}(X)
,

C_{*}(X_{(\leq 0)})\to C_{*}(X_{(\leq 1)})\to\cdots\to C_{*}(X_{(\leq\ell)})\to\cdots\to C_{*}(X)~.

The
E^{0}
page of the spectral sequence associated to this filtration is

E^{0}_{i,j}=C_{i+j}(X_{(\leq i)})/C_{i+j}(X_{(\leq i-1)})~.

Hence the
E^{1}
page is given by

E^{1}_{i,j}=H^{\text{red}}_{i+j}\left(C_{i+j}(X_{(\leq i)})/C_{i+j}(X_{(\leq i-1)}\right)\cong H^{\text{red}}_{i+j}\left(X_{(\leq i)}/X_{(\leq i-1)}\right)~,

where the homology is taken with respect to the
d^{1}
-differential.
Since
X
is compact and connected,
X_{(\leq i)}/X_{(\leq i-1)}\cong(X_{i})^{+}
, where

(X_{(i)})^{+}
denotes the one-point compactification of
X_{(i)}
. Further, since each stratum is equipped with a diffeomorphism
\alpha_{p}:X_{p}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{R}^{\mathsf{dim}(X_{p})}
,
we have the based homeomorphism,

X_{(i)}^{+}\cong_{\alpha_{p}^{+}}\left(\coprod_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})}\mathbb{R}^{i}\right)^{+}\cong\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})}S^{i}~.

(6)

Therefore,

E^{1}_{i,j}\cong H^{\text{red}}_{i+j}\left(\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})}S^{i}\right)\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\rangle,&\quad\text{if }j=0\\
0,&\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}~.

The
d^{1}
differential is then a homomorphism

d^{1}_{i,0}:\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i-1)})\rangle~.

We will now identify
d^{1}
with the homomorphism (
5
). This will complete the proof of the first
statement, since the spectral sequence collapses at the
E^{2}
page for dimension reasons.
Further, convergence of the spectral sequence
to
H_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
is seen from the fact that the filtration is finite.

The
d^{1}
differential is given by the map induced by applying
H_{*}^{\text{red}}
to the following composite morphism of spaces

\frac{X_{(\leq i)}}{X_{(\leq i-1)}}\simeq\mathsf{Cone}\left(X_{(\leq i-1)}\hookrightarrow X_{(\leq i)}\right)\to\Sigma X_{(\leq i-1)}^{+}\to\Sigma\frac{X_{(\leq i-1)}}{X_{(\leq i-2)}}~,

where the first morphism is given by collapsing
X_{(\leq i)}
in the cone, and the second morphism is the quotient.
Consider the continuous map

\gamma_{(\leq i-1)}^{!}:X_{(i)}^{+}\to\Sigma\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(\leq i-1)}}(X_{(\leq i)})^{+}~,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{100.1713pt}{-1.36389pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -26.19 -25.44 L 99.12 -25.44}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.83723pt}{-18.38869pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.88 3.32 C -2.35 1.33 -1.18 0.39 0 0 C -1.18 -0.39 -2.35 -1.33 -2.88 -3.32}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~,

Denote the indicated horizontal composite as
\gamma^{!}_{(i-1)}
. Since the bottom
composite induces the
d^{1}
differential upon applying reduced homology, we have identified the
d^{1}

differential with the map induced on homology by the composite
\Sigma\pi_{(i-1)}\circ\gamma^{!}_{(i-1)}.

Using the fact that for
Y
a locally compact and Hausdorff space, there is a based homeomorphism

\Sigma Y^{+}\cong(Y\times(0,1))^{+}
, we have the based homeomorphism

\displaystyle\Sigma\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)})^{+}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)})\times(0,1)\right)^{+}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\coprod_{\pi_{0}(\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)}))}\mathbb{R}^{i-1}\times(0,1)\right)^{+}

\displaystyle\cong\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)}))}S^{i}~.

Similar to the above identification we also have the following based homeomorphism

\Sigma X_{(i-1)}^{+}\cong\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i-1)})}S^{i}~.

Using these identifications together with Equation (
6
), we identify

\gamma^{!}_{(i-1)}
with a map between wedges of spheres

\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})}S^{i}\cong_{\alpha_{i}^{+}}X_{(i)}^{+}\xrightarrow{\gamma^{!}_{(i-1)}}\Sigma\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)})^{+}\xrightarrow{\cong}\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)}))}S^{i}~,

and we identify
\Sigma\pi_{(i-1)}
as a map between wedges of spheres,

\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)}))}S^{i}\cong\Sigma\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)})^{+}\xrightarrow{\Sigma\pi_{(i-1)}}\Sigma X_{(i-1)}^{+}\cong\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i-1)})}S^{i}~.

Thus, upon applying
H^{\text{red}}_{*}
, the
d^{1}
differential is the composite map

\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(\mathsf{Link}_{X_{(i-1)}}(X_{(i-1)}\cup X_{(i)})^{+})\rangle\to\mathbb{Z}\langle\pi_{0}(X_{(i-1)})\rangle~.

We can compute these induced maps via the degrees of the maps
\gamma^{!}_{(i-1)}
and
\Sigma\pi_{(i-1)}

(see
[
Hat02
]
for more details). To identify the
d^{1}
-differential with Equation (
5
),
and thus complete the proof, we now identify the degree of

\Sigma\pi_{(i-1)}\circ\gamma^{!}_{(i-1)}:\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})}S^{i}\to\bigvee_{\pi_{0}(X_{(i-1)})}S^{i}~,

with the formula in Equation (
5
).
The degree of a smooth map
f:X\to Y
between compact, oriented smooth manifolds can be computed by
choosing a regular value
y\in Y
, and then taking a count of preimages of
y
, signed according to whether

f
is orientation preserving or reversing at that point:

\mathsf{deg}(f)=\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}\mathsf{sgn}\left(\mathsf{det}(D_{x}f)\right)~,

(see
[
GP10
]
, for instance).
For ease of notation,
let us denote the composite
\Sigma\pi_{(i-1)}\circ\gamma^{!}_{(i-1)}
by
\Phi
. Note that
\Phi
is smooth
away from the basepoint. Further, each element of
\pi_{0}(X_{(i)})
is a stratum
X_{q}
, for some
q\in\mathcal{P}
,
such that
d(X_{q})=i
. Similarly, each element of
\pi_{0}(X_{(i-1)})
is a stratum
X_{p}
, for some
p\in\mathcal{P}
,
such that
d(X_{p})=i-1
. Thus, we can compute the degree by computing the degree of the induced map

\Phi_{p<q}:S^{i}_{q}:=X_{q}^{+}\to(X_{p}\times(0,1))^{+}=:S^{i}_{p}~,

for each
p<q\in\mathcal{P}
with
d(p)+1=d(q)
. Choosing a regular value,
y\in S^{i}_{p}
, we have

\mathsf{deg}(\Phi_{p<q})=\sum_{x\in\Phi_{p<q}^{-1}(y)}\mathsf{sgn}\left(\mathsf{det}(D_{x}\Phi_{p<q})\right)~.

Observing that
\mathsf{card}(\Phi_{p<q}^{-1}(y))=\mathsf{card}(\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{p<q}(X)))
, it just remains to establish that

\mathsf{sgn}(\mathsf{det}(D_{x}\Phi_{p<q}))=\mathsf{sgn}(\mathsf{det}(D_{\ell}\gamma\circ((D_{\ell}\pi)^{-1}\oplus\mathsf{id}_{(0,\varepsilon)})))~,

as in Equation (
5
). Since
\Phi_{p<q}
is given as the composite, we have

D_{x}\Phi_{p<q}=(D_{\ell}\pi\oplus\mathsf{id}_{(0,\varepsilon)})\circ(D_{x}\gamma)^{-1}~,

where
\ell=\gamma^{-1}_{p<q}(x)
. Hence the sign of this determinant matches that of Equation
(
5
), which completes the proof.
∎

4
The Schubert stratification

In Section
2.2
, we showed that the cells of the Grassmannian
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
are parameterized by the set

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}:=\{S\subset\{1,\dots,n\}~|~\mathsf{card}(S)=k\}~.

Since each element in
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
corresponds to a cell in the CW structure on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, there is a
canonical map

\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)\to\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}

given by sending a
k
-plane to the set indexing its
cell. This realizes
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
as a stratfied topological space with
S
-stratum the
S
-cell:

\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}:=\{V\in\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)~|~\text{$S$ is the maximal element in }{\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}}\text{ for which }V\in U_{S}\}~.

In particular, recall that each stratum of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
is diffeomorphic to Euclidean space.
In fact, as proven in a forthcoming paper, we prove:

Theorem 4.1
.

For
0\leq k\leq n
, the Schubert stratification of the Grassmannian
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
can be naturally upgraded to
the structure of a conically smooth stratified space, with respect to which, for each pair
S<T
in

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
with
d(S)+1=d(T)
, there are identifications
