{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{61.69653pt}{-1.29387pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{71.65207pt}{-1.47163pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}~,

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}_{S<T}

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left(\theta_{(i,j)}\right)\in[0,\pi]^{Z_{T}}\big|\text{ for all }(i,j)\in Z_{T},\text{ if }(i,j)\in Z_{S},\theta_{(i,j)}\in(0,\pi),\text{ else }\theta_{(i,j)}\in\{0,\pi\}\right\}

\displaystyle\cong(0,\pi)^{Z_{S}}\times\{0,\pi\}~.

As stated by Proposition
3.17
, to compute the homology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
, we need to understand
the differential given in Equation (
5
). In particular, for

S<T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
with
d(S)+1=d(T)
, we must understand the map

\sigma_{S}^{T}:\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{S<T})\to\{\pm 1\}

defined by sending
[\ell]\in\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{S<T})
to

\mathsf{det}\left(\mathbb{R}^{i}\oplus\mathbb{R}\cong T_{\pi_{S}^{T}(\ell)}\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}\oplus\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{(D_{\ell}\pi_{S}^{T})^{-1}\oplus\text{id}}T_{\ell}\mathsf{L}_{S<T}\oplus\mathbb{R}\cong T_{(\ell,\varepsilon/2)}(\mathsf{L}_{S<T}\times(0,\varepsilon))\xrightarrow{D_{\ell}\gamma_{S}^{T}}T_{\gamma_{S}^{T}(\ell)}\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{T}\cong\mathbb{R}^{i+1}\right)

=\mathsf{det}\left((D_{\ell}\pi_{S}^{T})^{-1}\right)\cdot\mathsf{det}\left(D_{\ell}\gamma_{S}^{T}\right)~.

So, let us fix
S<T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
with
d(S)+1=d(T)
.

The following immediate consequence of Corollary
4.1
tells us that we only need to compute

\sigma^{T}_{S}
for two values.

Corollary 4.2
.

For
S<T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
with
d(S)+1=d(T)
,
\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{S<T})\cong\{0,\pi\}
.

Let us fix two such values, one in each connected component of
\mathsf{L}_{S<T}
:

\Theta_{S<T}^{0}=(\theta^{0}_{(i,j)})_{(i,j)\in Z_{T}}~,\qquad\theta^{0}_{(i,j)}:=\begin{cases}\pi/2,&\text{ if }(i,j)\in Z_{S}\\
0,&\text{ if }(i,j)\in Z_{T}\setminus Z_{S}\end{cases}~,

and

\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}=(\theta^{\pi}_{(i,j)})_{(i,j)\in Z_{T}}~,\qquad\theta^{\pi}_{(i,j)}:=\begin{cases}\pi/2,&\text{ if }(i,j)\in Z_{S}\\
\pi,&\text{ if }(i,j)\in Z_{T}\setminus Z_{S}\end{cases}~.

We will now unpack the maps
D_{\ell}\pi_{S}^{T}
and
D_{\ell}\gamma_{S}^{T}
. Let us now fix
S<T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}

with
d(S)+1=d(T)
.
Let
M\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
be the
maximal element.

We have the following commutative diagrams of spaces:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{L}_{S<T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(0,\pi\right)^{Z_{S}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{V}_{k}^{o}(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{S<T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inc}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Rot}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{col}}

,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{L}_{S<T}\times(0,\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(0,\pi\right)^{Z_{T}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{V}_{k}^{o}(n)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{S<T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{swap}\amalg\mathsf{Rswap}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{inc}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Rot}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{col}}
,

from which, we compute
D_{\ell}\pi_{S<T}
and
D_{\ell}\gamma_{S<T}
as their respective composites.

Lemma 4.3
.

For
S<T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
with
d(S)+1=d(T)
,

\sigma_{S}^{T}([\ell])=\begin{cases}(-1)^{1+k+i_{S<T}+\sum\limits_{i=i_{S<T}+1}^{k}s_{i}-i},&\quad\text{if }\ell\in[0]\in\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{S<T})\\
(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i=i_{S<T}}^{k}s_{i}-i},&\quad\text{if }\ell\in[\pi]\in\pi_{0}(\mathsf{L}_{S<T})\end{cases}~,

where
1\leq i_{S<T}\leq k
is the index for which
S_{i_{S<T}}+1=T_{i_{S<T}}
.

Proof.

Let us first consider the derivative of
\mathsf{col}
.
For
A\in V_{k}^{o}(n)
,
T_{A}V_{k}^{o}(n)=\{V\in\mathsf{Mat}_{n\times k}~|~V^{T}A+A^{T}V=0\}
,
and
T_{\mathsf{col}(A)}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n))\cong\hom(\mathsf{col}(A),\mathsf{col}(A)^{\perp})\cong\mathsf{Mat}_{(n-k)\times k}
. Thus, a basis
for
T_{\mathsf{col}(A)}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n))
is given by the set
\mathcal{M}:=\{\mathsf{M}_{(i,j)}~|~1\leq i\leq n\setminus k,~1\leq j\leq k\}
, where
\mathsf{M}_{(i,j)}
has a 1 in the
(i,j)
entry and all other entries are 0.
Thus, for
\mathsf{col}(A)\in\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S}
,
T_{\mathsf{col}(A)}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)_{S})
has a basis given by

\mathcal{M}_{S}:=\{M_{(i,j)}~|~1\leq j\leq k,~1\leq i<s_{j}\}\subset\mathcal{M}.

Note that we will refer to elements of
\mathcal{M}
or
\mathcal{M}_{S}
as the indexing pairs,
(i,j)
.Recall that
\mathsf{col}:V_{k}^{o}(n\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
takes an
n\times k

matrix to its column space. Let
A\in\{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{0}),\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi})\}
.
The surjective submersion
O(n)\to V_{k}^{o}(n)
tells us that for each

V\in T_{A}V_{k}^{o}(n)
, there
exists
\widetilde{V}\in\mathsf{Skew}(n)
for which
e^{t\widetilde{V}}A
represents the equivalence class of the
tangent vector
V
.
Let
\mathbbm{1}_{(\underline{n}\setminus S)\times n}
denote the
n\times n
identity matrix with the rows labeled by
S
removed.
Now, in local coordinates, we can express
\mathsf{col}
as the matrix

\mathbbm{1}_{(\underline{n}\setminus S)\times n}e^{t\widetilde{V}}A(A^{T}e^{t\widetilde{V}}A)^{-1}~,

whose derivative at
t=0
is

\frac{d}{dt}\bigg|_{t=0}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{(\underline{n}\setminus S)\times n}e^{t\widetilde{V}}A(A^{T}e^{t\widetilde{V}}A)^{-1}\right)=\mathbbm{1}_{(\underline{n}\setminus S)\times n}\widetilde{V}A~.

When
A=\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{0})
,
\widetilde{V}A=V
, so
D_{\Theta_{S<T}^{0}}\mathsf{col}:V_{k}^{o}(n)\to\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)

sends
V
to the
\underline{n}\setminus S\times k
matrix that consists of the
\underline{n}\setminus S
rows of
V
. When

A=\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi})
,
\widetilde{V}A
is
V
except with the
i_{S<T}
column negated.
Thus,
D_{\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}}\mathsf{col}
sends
V
to the matrix that consists of the
\underline{n}\setminus S
rows of
V
, but
with the
i_{S<T}
column negated. Note that

T_{A}V_{k}^{o}(n)=\{V\in\mathsf{Mat}_{n\times k}~|~V^{T}A+A^{T}V=0\}
has a natural basis consisting of two types
of matrices. The first type consists of matrices whose only nonzero entry is the
(i,j)
entry, for

(i,j)\not\in S\times S
, one such matrix for each such
(i,j)
.
The second type of matrices will have their nonzero entries concentrated in the rows and columns labeled by
S
.
Note that our description of the derivative shows that for both
0
and
\pi
, the derivative sends all matrices of
the second type to 0. Note that
T_{\mathsf{col}(A)}\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)\cong\mathsf{Mat}_{(n-k)\times k}
has a natural
basis consisting of the matrices of the first type described above. Thus, deleting the second type of basis elements
of
T_{A}V_{k}^{o}(n)
,
D_{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{0})}\mathsf{col}
is simply the identity matrix, and

D_{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi})}
is the identity matrix except with all columns labeled by
(i,j)
for which

j=i_{S<T}
. Finally, since we are only interested in those basis elements for which
(i,j)\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
,
as discussed above, we conclude that

\det(D_{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{0})}\mathsf{col})=1~,

and

\det(D_{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi})}\mathsf{col})=(-1)^{\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in\mathcal{M}_{S^{c}}^{*}~|~j=i_{S<T}\}}=(-1)^{s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}}~.

Note that

\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta^{0}_{S<T})=\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}(\Theta^{\pi}_{S<T})\in V^{o}_{k}(n)
is the
n\times k
matrix whose
i
th
column is
\mathbf{e}_{s_{i}}
.
We will first consider

D_{\Theta^{0}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}=\left[\left(\frac{\partial{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}}}{\partial\theta_{(i,j)}}\right)_{xy}\right]~.

That is, for each pair,
(i,j)\in Z_{S}
and
(x,y)\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
, we will identify the
xy
-entry in the
matrix
\frac{\partial{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}}}{\partial\theta_{(i,j)}}
. Note that we ignore those entries indexed by elements

(x,y)\in\mathcal{M}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{S}
, as
D\mathsf{col}
will map those to 0, as discussed above.
Let us now fix
(i,j)\in Z_{S}
. Then

\frac{\partial{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}}}{\partial\theta_{(i,j)}}(\Theta_{S<T}^{0})=\left(\prod_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)}\mathsf{R}_{j^{\prime}}(\theta_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})})\right)D\mathsf{R}_{j}(\theta_{(i,j)})\left(\prod_{(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})>(i,j)}\mathsf{R}_{j^{\prime\prime}}(\theta_{(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})})\right)~.

For ease of notation, let us denote

\mathsf{Rot}^{<(i,j)}_{S}:=\prod_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})<(i,j)}\mathsf{R}_{j^{\prime}}(\theta_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})})~,

and

\mathsf{Rot}^{>(i,j)}_{S}:=\prod_{(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})>(i,j)}\mathsf{R}_{j^{\prime\prime}}(\theta_{(i^{\prime\prime},j^{\prime\prime})})~.

Recall that
\theta_{(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})}=\pi/2
for each
(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in Z_{S}
, so each entry in
D\mathsf{R}_{j}(\theta_{(i,j)})
is zero,
except for the
(j,j)
and
(j+1,j+1)
entries which are both
-1
. Thus, we only need to know which
\mathbf{e}_{r}
gets
sent to
\mathbf{e}_{j}
and
\mathbf{e}_{j+1}
under the map
\mathsf{Rot}^{>(i,j)}_{S}
. By definition,
\mathsf{Rot}^{>(i,j)}_{S}

is a product of cyclic permutation matrices

[\mathbf{e}_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{j}][\mathbf{e}_{i+1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i+1}}]\cdots[\mathbf{e}_{k}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{k}}]~.

The first block
[\mathbf{e}_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{j}]
sends

\mathbf{e}_{r}\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{j},&\text{ if }r=i\\
\mathbf{e}_{j+1},&\text{ if }r=j+1\end{cases}~.

For the next block,
[\mathbf{e}_{i+1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i+1}}]
, we care about what gets sent to
\mathbf{e}_{i}
and
\mathbf{e}_{j+1}
.
We see that
[\mathbf{e}_{i+1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i+1}}]
sends

\mathbf{e}_{r}\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{i},&\text{ if }r=i\\
-\mathbf{e}_{j+1},&\text{ if }r=j+2\end{cases}~.

Similarly, the next block will fix
\mathbf{e}_{i}
, and send
\mathbf{e}_{j+3}
to
-\mathbf{e}_{j+2}
. Since there are
k-i
blocks after

[\mathbf{e}_{i}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{j}]
, we see that

D\mathsf{R}_{j}\circ\mathsf{Rot}^{>(i,j)}_{S}:\mathbf{e}_{r}\mapsto\begin{cases}-\mathbf{e}_{j},&\text{ if }r=i\\
(-1)^{k-i+1}\mathbf{e}_{j+1},&\text{ if }r=j+k-i+1\\
0,&\text{ else}\end{cases}~.

Next, we will determine where
\mathsf{Rot}^{<(i,j)}_{S}
sends
\mathbf{e}_{j}
and
\mathbf{e}_{j+1}
. Note that

\widehat{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
is the following product of cyclic permutation matrices

[\mathbf{e}_{1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{1}}]\cdots[\mathbf{e}_{i-1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i-1}}][\mathbf{e}_{j+1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i}}].

The
[\mathbf{e}_{j+1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i}}]
block fixes
\mathbf{e}_{j}
and sends
\mathbf{e}_{j+1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i}}
. The next block from
the right,
[\mathbf{e}_{i-1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i-1}}]
sends

\mathbf{e}_{j}\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{j},&\text{ if }j>s_{i-1}\\
-\mathbf{e}_{j-1},&\text{ else}\end{cases}~.

Similarly, the next block sends

\mathbf{e}_{j-1}\mapsto\begin{cases}\mathbf{e}_{j-1},&\text{ if }j-1>s_{i-2}\\
-\mathbf{e}_{j-2},&\text{ else}\end{cases}~.

Letting
\beta_{S}(i,j):=\mathsf{card}\{1\leq\ell<i~|~j+1-\ell\leq s_{i-\ell}\}
, we see that
\widehat{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
sends

\mathbf{e}_{j}\mapsto(-1)^{\beta}\mathbf{e}_{j-\beta}
and
\mathbf{e}_{j+1}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{s_{i}}.
Therefore, the composite

\frac{\partial{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}}}{\partial\theta_{(i,j)}}(\Theta_{S<T}^{0})
is given by

\mathbf{e}_{i}\mapsto(-1)^{\beta+1}\mathbf{e}_{j-\beta}
and
\mathbf{e}_{j+k-i+1}\mapsto(-1)^{k-i+1}\mathbf{e}_{s_{i}}
. The
(i,j)

column of

D_{\Theta^{0}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}=\left[\left(\frac{\partial{\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}}}{\partial\theta_{(i,j)}}\right)_{xy}\right]

has a single nonzero entry of
(-1)^{\beta+1}
in the
(j-\beta,i)\in\mathcal{M}_{S}
row. To compute the
determinant of
D_{\Theta^{0}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
, we first observe that for
(i,j)<(i,j-1)\in Z_{S}
, there is an inequality,

j-1-\beta_{S}(i,j-1)<j-\beta_{S}(i,j)
. Therefore, ignoring signs,
D_{\Theta^{0}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
, is a block sum of
antidiagonal matrices. There will be a block of size
s_{i}-i
for each
1\leq i\leq k
, so ignoring signs, the determinant
of
D_{\Theta^{0}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
is

(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{s_{i}-i-1}r}~.

The number of negative entries of
D_{\Theta^{0}_{S<T}}
is given by

\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}(\beta_{S}(i,j)+1)=d(S)+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\beta_{S}(i,j)~.

All told,

\det(D_{\Theta^{0}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S})=(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{s_{i}-i-1}r}(-1)^{d(S)+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\beta_{S}(i,j)}~.

The only difference in computing
\det(D_{\Theta^{\pi}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S})

is that the matrix in the
(i_{S<T},S_{i_{S<T}})
slot is no longer the identity matrix, but rather the diagonal
matrix all of whose diagronal entries are
1
, except the
(i_{S<T},i_{S<T})
and
(i_{S<T}+1,i_{S<T}+1)

entries are both
-1
. For
(i,j)\in Z_{S}
, if
(i,j)<(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})
, then
i<i_{S<T}
, and thus

\overline{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
still sends
e_{i}\mapsto e_{j}
as in the
\Theta_{S<T}^{0}
case.
Let us now consider the case
(i,j)>(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})
. If
i=i_{S<T}
, then the effect on

\widehat{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
is that the
i_{S<T}
block
now sends
\mathbf{e}_{j+1}\mapsto-\mathbf{e}_{s_{i_{S<T}}}
and
\mathbf{e}_{i_{S<T}+1}\mapsto-\mathbf{e}_{i_{S<T}+1}
. Thus,
this block sends
\mathbf{e}_{j}\mapsto\mathbf{e}_{j}
as in the
\Theta^{0}_{S<T}
case. The last case to consider is that

i>i_{S<T}
. The effect on
\widehat{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
is that the
i_{S<T}
block now sends

\mathbf{e}_{i_{S<T}}\mapsto-\mathbf{e}_{i_{S<T}}
and
\mathbf{e}_{i_{S<T}+1}\mapsto-\mathbf{e}_{i_{S<T}+1}
. This will introduce an
extra factor of
-1
on the image
\mathbf{e}_{j}
precisely if
j+1-(i-i_{S<T})=s_{i_{S<T}}+1
,
or more simply if
j-i=s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}
. Thus, the determinant of
D_{\Theta^{\pi}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S}
will have
one extra factor of
-1
for each pair
(i,j)\in Z_{S}
for which
j-i=s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}
. Note that

\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{S}~|~j-i=s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}\}=k-i_{S<T}~.

Therefore,

\det(D_{\Theta^{\pi}_{S<T}}\widetilde{\mathsf{Rot}}_{S})=(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{s_{i}-i-1}r}(-1)^{d(S)+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\beta_{S}(i,j)}(-1)^{k-i_{S<T}}~.

Recall that
\mathsf{swap}:(0,\pi)^{Z_{S}}\times\{0\}\times(0,\pi)
simply moves the last coordinate to the

(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})
coordinate in
Z_{T}
. Thus,
the derivative of
\mathsf{swap}
is a matrix consisting of
1
’s along the diagonal until the

(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})
row.
The
(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})
row will consist of all zeros, except the final column will be a
1
. There will be
1
’s
along the subdiagonal, and all other entries are 0. Likewise, the derivative of
\mathsf{Rswap}
will be the same as
the derivative of
\mathsf{swap}
, except the last column of the
(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})
row will be a
-1
, since

\mathsf{Rswap}
is orientation reversing in that factor. Thus,

\det(D_{\Theta_{S<T}^{0}}\mathsf{swap})=(-1)^{\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{T}~|~(i,j)>(I_{S<T},s_{I_{S<T}})\}}~,

and

\det(D_{\Theta_{S<T}^{0}}\mathsf{Rswap})=(-1)^{\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{T}~|~(i,j)>(I_{S<T},s_{I_{S<T}})\}+1}~.

All told, we have

\displaystyle\sigma^{T}_{S}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{s_{i}-i-1}r+d(S)+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\beta_{S}(i,j)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{t_{i}-i-1}r+d(T)+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{T}}\beta_{T}(i,j)+\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{T}~|~(i,j)>(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})\}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{1+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{s_{i}-i-1}r+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\beta_{S}(i,j)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{t_{i}-i-1}r+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{T}}\beta_{T}(i,j)+\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{T}~|~(i,j)>(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})\}}~,

since
d(S)+d(T)=2d(S)+1
.
Notice that for
i\neq i_{S<T}
,

\sum\limits_{r=1}^{t_{i}-i-1}r=\sum\limits_{r=1}^{s_{i}-i-1}r~.

Further,
t_{i_{S<T}}=s_{i_{S<T}}+1
.
Thus,

(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{s_{i}-i-1}r+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k}\sum\limits_{r=1}^{t_{i}-i-1}r}=(-1)^{s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}}~.

Next, note that

\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{T}~|~(i,j)>(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})\}=\sum\limits_{i=i_{S<T}}^{k}(s_{i}-i)~.

To assess when
\beta_{S}(i,j)\neq\beta_{T}(i,j)
, we must figure out if there exists
1\leq\ell<i
for which

j+1-\ell\leq t_{i-\ell}
, yet
j+1-\ell>s_{i-\ell}
. Since
s_{i}=t_{i}
for all
i
except
i=i_{S<T}
, the only
\ell

for which this could possibly hold is
\ell=i-i_{S<T}
. Then, we are seeking
(i,j)\in Z_{S}
for which

j-i+i_{S<T}+1\leq t_{i_{S<T}}=s_{i_{S<T}}+1
and
j-i+i_{S<T}+1>s_{i_{S<T}}
. Both of these inequalities
hold precisely if
j-i=s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}
. Therefore,

(-1)^{\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{S}}\beta_{S}(i,j)+\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in Z_{T}}\beta_{T}(i,j)}=(-1)^{\beta_{T}(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})+\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{S}~|~j-i=s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}\}}=(-1)^{k-i_{S<T}}~,

since
\beta_{T}(i_{S<T},s_{i_{S<T}})=0
, and
\mathsf{card}\{(i,j)\in Z_{S}~|~j-i=s_{i_{S<T}}-i_{S<T}\}=k-i_{S<T}
.
Hence, the formula for
\sigma^{T}_{S}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])
is proven. The above reductions also yield the stated
formula for
\sigma^{T}_{S}([\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}])
.
∎

Observation 4.4
.

We can further simplify the boundary formula

\displaystyle\sigma_{S}^{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])+\sigma_{S}^{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}])

\displaystyle=\sum(-1)^{1+k+i_{S<T}+\sum\limits_{i=i_{S<T}+1}^{k}s_{i}-i}+(-1)^{\sum\limits_{i=i_{S<T}}^{k}s_{i}-i}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{i_{S<T}+\sum_{i=i_{S<T}+1}^{k}(s_{i}-i)}\left((-1)^{(s_{i_{S<T}})}-(-1)^{k}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\sum_{i=i_{S<T}}^{k}(s_{i}-i)}\left(1-(-1)^{k-s_{i_{S<T}}}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\sum_{i=i_{S<T}}^{k}(s_{i}-i)}2,&\text{ if }S<T\text{ and }k\not\equiv s_{i_{S<T}}\mod 2\\
0,&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}~.

Observation 4.5

(Cohomology)
.

Lemma
4.3
gives a simple easy to compute formula for the boundary maps in

C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})
, the integral chain complex of the Schubert CW structure on
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
. Note that this
gives us a description of
C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})
, the integral chain complex. The
i
-th chain group is
generated by the same generators of
i
-th chain group, and now the differential

C^{i}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})\to C^{i+1}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})
is simply the transpose of the differential

C_{i+1}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})\to C_{i}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})
. In other words, given
S<T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
with

d(T)=d(S)+1
, the coefficient
\sigma^{S}_{T}=\sigma^{T}_{S}
.
For each
0\leq r\leq k
, consider the map

d_{r}:\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\xrightarrow{\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}\mapsto\sum_{r\leq i\leq k}s_{i}-i}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}~.

The differential
\partial^{i}:C^{i}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})\to C^{i+1}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})
is given by

\partial^{i}:S=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}~\mapsto~\sum\limits_{r\in\left\{1\leq r\leq k~\mid~s_{r+1}-s_{r}>1\text{ and }k-s_{r}\text{ is odd}\right\}}(-1)^{d_{r-1}(S)}2\cdot S_{r}~,

(7)

where
S_{r}:=\{s_{1}<\cdots<s_{r-1}<s_{r}+1<s_{r+1}<\cdots<s_{k}\}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
.

Observation 4.6

(Arbitrary coefficients)
.

Thus, for the chain complex with coefficients in a commutative ring
R
, we see that

C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)
has the same underlying chain groups, and differential specified by

\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])+\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}])=\begin{cases}(-1)^{\sum_{i=i_{S<T}}^{k}(s_{i}-i)}2,&\text{ if }S<T\text{ and }k\not\equiv s_{i_{S<T}}\mod 2\\
0,&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}~.

Thus, if
0=2
in
R
, the differentials are all
0
, which in particular, recovers the case of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

coefficients, e.g.
[
MS75
]
.
These computations should also specialize to agree with those of
[
Bor53
]
and
[
Tak62
]
, though we do
not verify this.

Figure 3:
A visual of the chain complex for
\mathsf{Gr}_{3}(6)
. Underlying this figure is the poset
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{6}{3}
,
with each relation arrow labeled by the coefficient of the differential.

5
The cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)

The visualization of
C^{\ast}(\mathsf{Gr}_{6}(12);\mathbb{Z})
from Figures
4
and
6
suggests the chain complex
can be written as a finite direct sum of cubes.
We will now prove this remarkable observation in Theorem
5.2
.
A consequence of this theorem is a closed formula for the
R
-cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
,
which is proven in Corollary
5.7
.

For
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, define

\mathsf{In}(S):=\{i\in\underline{k}~|~s_{i}\equiv k~(\text{mod }2)\text{ and }s_{i-1}<s_{i}-1\}\subset\underline{k}~,

and

\mathsf{Out}(S):=\{i\in\underline{k}~|~s_{i}\not\equiv k~(\text{mod }2)\text{ and }s_{i}+1<s_{i+1}\}\subset\underline{k}~,

where we set
s_{0}:=0
, and
s_{k+1}:=n+1
, for notational purposes. Further, define

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}:=\{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}~|~\mathsf{Out}(S)=\emptyset\}~,\qquad\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}:=\{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}~|~\mathsf{In}(S)=\emptyset\}~,

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Min}}:=\{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}~|~\mathsf{Min}(\mathsf{In}(S)\cup\mathsf{Out}(S))\in\mathsf{In}(S)\}.

Consider the subposet
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}\subset\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
consisting of the same objects as

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
, yet only those relations
S<T
that factor as a sequence of relations

S=U_{0}<U_{1}<\cdots<U_{\ell}=T
in which for all
0<r\leq\ell
, we have

d(U_{r})-d(U_{r-1})=1
and
i_{U_{r-1}<U_{r}}\not\equiv k~\mathsf{mod}~2
.
This subposet is generated by the relations
S<T
for which
d(T)-d(S)=1
and

\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])+\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}])\neq 0
.

Observation 5.1
.

Let
S<T
be a relation in
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
. This relation belongs to
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}
if and
only if
s_{i}=t_{i}-1
for all
i\in\mathsf{Out}(S)\cap\mathsf{In}(T)
and
s_{i}=t_{i}
for all
i\not\in\mathsf{Out}(S)\cap\mathsf{In}(T)
.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. There is an isomorphism of chain complexes

\displaystyle C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\otimes\mathsf{In}(S)}[d(S)-\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))]

\displaystyle\cong\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}\mathbb{Z}[d(S)]\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Min}}}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[d(S)-1]\right)

Proof.

The first isomorphism is immediate from Lemmas
5.3
and
5.4
.
The first summand of the second isomorphism comes from the

S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
such that
\mathsf{In}(S)=\emptyset
. The second factor follows from
5.5
.
∎

See Figure
4
for a depiction of
C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{5}(10);R)
and Figure
5
for a depiction of the
isomorphic complex

\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{10}{5}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}\mathbb{Z}[d(S)]\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{10}{5}_{\mathsf{Min}}}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[d(S)-1]\right)~.

We now prove the lemmas that yield the proof of Theorem
5.2
.

Lemma 5.3
.

There is an isomorphism of chain complexes

C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);\mathbb{Z})\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}}C_{*}(\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{In}(S))^{\mathsf{op}};\mathbb{Z})~.

Proof.

We can regard

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}
as a weighted level graph with weights given by the coefficients

\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])+\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}])
, and the level given by the dimension of
the corresponding Schubert cells. Recall there is a bijection between weighted level graphs whose adjacency
matrices square to the zero matrix and chain complexes. Since
C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n))
is a chain complex, and

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}
precisely selects out the relations
S<T
with

\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])+\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}])\neq 0
, the adjacency matrix of

(\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}})^{\mathsf{op}}
squares to the zero matrix. Let us denote the chain complex associated to

(\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}})^{\mathsf{op}}
by
C_{*}((\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}})^{\mathsf{op}})
. Thus, by construction,

C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n))\cong C_{*}((\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}})^{\mathsf{op}})
.
Next, we claim the canonical functor between posets

\coprod_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}}\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S}\to\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}

is an isomorphism. To prove this, note that for each
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}
, the canonical inclusion

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S}\hookrightarrow\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}
is fully faithful. Thus, to prove the
claim, it is enough to show that for each
T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}
, there exists a unique

S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}
such that
T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S}
. So, for

T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}
, define a set
S^{T}\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}
by defining

S^{T}_{i}=T_{i}
in
i\not\in\mathsf{Out}(T)
, and
S^{T}_{i}=T_{i}+1
otherwise. By construction,

S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}
. Observation
5.1
implies that
T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S^{T}}
,
and further that
S^{T}
is the unique element of
\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}
for which this is true.
Now, observe that for
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}
, there is an isomorphism of posets

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\leq S}^{\mathsf{adm}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{In}(S))
given by sending
T\mapsto\mathsf{Out}(T)
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle C_{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n))

\displaystyle\cong C_{*}\left((\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}})^{\mathsf{op}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong C_{*}\left(\coprod_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}}(\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S})^{\mathsf{op}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong C_{*}\left(\coprod_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}}\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{In}(S))^{\mathsf{op}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}}C_{*}\left(\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{In}(S))^{\mathsf{op}}\right)~.

∎

Lemma 5.4
.

There is aa isomorphism of chain complexes

C_{*}(\mathsf{Sub}(\mathsf{In}(S)^{\mathsf{op}})\cong\mathsf{Cone}\left(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}\right)^{\otimes\mathsf{In}(S)}~.

Proof.

In terms of their associated weighted level graphs, note that both underlying digraphs are isomorphic to the poset

\mathsf{Sub}(\{1<\cdots<\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))\})
. Further, the weights in both weighted level graphs are all
\pm 2
.
We will now prove that for any cardinality
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, any two choices of weights drawn from the
set
\{\pm 2\}
on the
digraph
\mathsf{Sub}(\underline{r})
, such that their adjacency matrices square to 0, are isomorphic. In fact, proceeding
by induction on
r
, we only need to prove the case
r=2
, as the cases
r=0,1
are clear.
Namely, we will show that given any solid diagrams with weights
u_{*}^{*},v_{*}^{*}\in\{\pm 2\}
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1,2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1,2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{2\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\emptyset}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}^{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}^{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{1}^{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{2}^{12}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\emptyset}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{\emptyset}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{\emptyset}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\emptyset}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{\emptyset}^{2}}

there exists filler arrows,
f_{\emptyset},f_{1},f_{2},f_{12}\in\{\pm 1\}
. Further,
f_{1},f_{2}
, and
f_{12}
are uniquely
determined by
f_{\emptyset}
. The commutivity conditions give the follwing four equations:

f_{1}u_{\emptyset}^{1}=v_{\emptyset}^{1}f_{\emptyset}

(8a)

f_{2}u_{\emptyset}^{2}=v_{\emptyset}^{2}f_{\emptyset}

(8b)

f_{12}u_{1}^{12}=v_{1}^{12}f_{1}

(9a)

f_{12}u_{2}^{12}=v_{2}^{12}f_{2}~.

(9b)

The fact that each solid diagram defines a chain complex results in the following two equations:

u_{1}^{12}u_{\emptyset}^{1}=u_{2}^{12}u_{\emptyset}^{2}

(10a)

v_{1}^{12}v_{\emptyset}^{1}=v_{2}^{12}v_{\emptyset}^{2}~.

(10b)

Equations
8a
and
8b
uniquely determine
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
in terms of
f_{\emptyset}
.
Multplying Equation
8a
on the left by
v_{1}^{12}
and substituting in Equations
9a
and
10b

yields
f_{12}u_{1}^{12}u_{\emptyset}^{1}=v_{2}^{12}v_{\emptyset}^{2}f_{\emptyset}
, which uniquely determines

f_{12}
in terms of
f_{\emptyset}
. It remains to show that this value of
f_{12}
is compatible with the
value determined by Equation
9b
. Multiplying Equation
8b
on the left by
v_{2}^{12}
and
substituting in Equations
9b
and
10a
also yields the equation

f_{12}u_{1}^{12}u_{\emptyset}^{1}=v_{2}^{12}v_{\emptyset}^{2}f_{\emptyset}
. Thus, there is a consistent value
of
f_{12}
uniquely determined by
f_{\emptyset}
.
∎

Lemma 5.5
.

For
r>0
, there is an isomorphism of chain complexes

\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\otimes r}\cong\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq r-1}\left(\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[a]\right)^{\oplus{r-1\choose a}}~.

Proof.

We will prove this by induction on
r
. As
r=1
is clear, consider the case
r=2
. The following diagram
exhibits such an isomorphism:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Assume
r>1
, then

\displaystyle\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\otimes r}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\otimes r-1}

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})\otimes\left(\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq r-2}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[a]^{\oplus{r-2\choose a}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq r-2}\left(\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})\otimes\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})\right)[a]^{\oplus{r-2\choose a}}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq r-2}\left(\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})\oplus\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})\right)[a]^{\oplus{r-2\choose a}}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq r-2}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[a]^{\oplus{r-2\choose a}}\right)\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq r-2}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[a+1]^{\oplus{r-2\choose a}}\right)

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{0<a\leq r-2}\left(\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus{r-2\choose a}}\oplus\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})^{\oplus{r-2\choose a-1}}\right)[a]\right)\oplus\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[r-1]

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{0\leq a\leq r-1}\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z})[a]^{\oplus{r-1\choose a}}~.

∎

We now prove several corollaries of Theorem
5.2
, that, in particular,
provide a closed formula for the
R
-cohomology of
\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n)
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
R
be a commutative ring. There is an isomorphism of chain complexes

\displaystyle C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)\cong

\displaystyle\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\cap\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}R[-d(s)]\right)

\displaystyle\oplus\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}\bigoplus_{0\leq a<\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))}\mathsf{Cone}(R\xrightarrow{2}R)^{\oplus{\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))-1\choose a}}[-d(S)+a]\right)~.

Proof.

This follows from the prior lemmas, and observing that

\mathsf{Hom}(\mathsf{Cone}(\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{2}\mathbb{Z}),R)\cong\mathsf{Cone}(R\xrightarrow{2}R)[-1]~.

∎

Corollary 5.7
.

There is an isomorphism of graded
R
-modules

H^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}}V_{S}[-d(S)]~,

where

V_{S}:=\begin{cases}R,&\text{ if }\mathsf{In}(S)=\emptyset=\mathsf{Out}(S)\\
\mathsf{ker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R),&\text{ if }\mathsf{Min}(\mathsf{In}(S)\cup\mathsf{Out}(S))\in\mathsf{Out}(S)\\
\mathsf{coker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R),&\text{ if }\mathsf{Min}(\mathsf{In}(S)\cup\mathsf{Out}(S))\in\mathsf{In}(S)\end{cases}.

Proof.

Taking the cohomology of the chain complex in Corollary
5.6
yields

\displaystyle H^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)

\displaystyle\cong\left(\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\cap\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}R[d(S)]\right)

\displaystyle\oplus\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}\bigoplus_{0\leq a<\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))}H^{*}\left(0\to R[a]\xrightarrow{2}R[a-1]\to 0\right)[-d(S)+\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))]^{\oplus{\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))-1\choose a}}~.

Note that

\displaystyle H^{*}

\displaystyle\left(0\to R[a]\xrightarrow{2}R[a-1]\to 0\right)[-d(S)+\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))]

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{ker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R)[-d(S)+\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))-a]\oplus\mathsf{coker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R)[-d(S)+\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))-a-1]~.

Thus, the number of summands in

\bigoplus_{0\leq a<\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))}H^{*}\left(0\to R[a]\xrightarrow{2}R[a+1]\to 0\right)[d(S)-\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))]^{\oplus{\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))-1\choose a}}

is equal to
2^{\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))}
. Hence there is one summand for each set in

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S}
. Let us choose the following convention: Assign to each

T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S}
, the
R
-module

T\mapsto V_{T}:=\begin{cases}\mathsf{ker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R),&\text{ if }\mathsf{Min}(\mathsf{In}(T)\cup\mathsf{Out}(T))\in\mathsf{Out}(T)\\
\mathsf{coker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R),&\text{ if }\mathsf{Min}(\mathsf{In}(T)\cup\mathsf{Out}(T))\in\mathsf{In}(T)\end{cases}.

This choice amounts to the following: Consider the weighted level graph assoicatied to the complex in the description of

C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)
from Corollary
5.6
. See Figure
5
for the example of
\mathsf{Gr}_{5}(10)
.
We assign
\mathsf{ker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R)
to the start node of each edge, and
\mathsf{coker}(R\xrightarrow{2}R)
to the end
node of each edge.
Recall from the proof of Proposition
5.3
that for each
T
, there is a unique
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
such that

T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}\bigoplus_{0\leq a<\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))}

\displaystyle H^{*}\left(0\to R[a]\xrightarrow{2}R[a-1]\to 0\right)[-d(S)+\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))]^{\oplus{\mathsf{card}(\mathsf{In}(S))-1\choose a}}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}}\bigoplus_{T\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}^{\mathsf{adm}}_{\leq S}}V_{T}[-d(T)]

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}\setminus\left(\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{Out}}\setminus\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}_{\mathsf{In}}\right)}V_{S}[-d(S)]~.

Extending the assignment
S\mapsto V_{S}
by defining
V_{S}:=R
if
\mathsf{In}(S)=\emptyset=\mathsf{Out}(S)
, yields the
desired result:

H^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{k}(n);R)\cong\bigoplus_{S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}}V_{S}[-d(S)]
.
∎

6
Computations

Finally, we present some computations for various
n
and
k
.
Figure
3
provides a graphical depiction of the chain complex of
\mathsf{Gr}_{3}(6)
. The vertical columns
list the generators of
C^{i}(\mathsf{Gr}_{3}(6))
in terms of increasing dimension, with the generator of
C^{0}(\mathsf{Gr}_{3}(6))
on
the left, and the generator of
C^{6}(\mathsf{Gr}_{3}(6))
on the right. The arrows specify the poset structure of the poset,

\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{6}{3}
, which stratifies the space
\mathsf{Gr}_{3}(6)
. Each arrow,
S\to T
, is labeled by the coefficient of its
differential:
\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{0}])+\sigma^{S}_{T}([\Theta_{S<T}^{\pi}])
.
Figures
4
and
6
give a graphical depiction of
C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{5}(10);\mathbb{Z})
and

C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{6}(12);\mathbb{Z})
, respectively.
Each node in the images represents a set
S\in\textstyle\genfrac{}{}{0.0pt}{}{n}{k}
. To make the images more legible,
in Figure
4
, the nodes have been labeled using base 11 notation, where
a=10
, and we left the nodes
unlabeled in Figure
6
.
The blue dotted arrows indicate the coefficient of the differential is
-2

and the solid red lines indicate the coefficient of the differential is
+2
.
Figure
5
is a depiction of the chain complex from Theorem
5.2
that is isomorphic to
C^{*}(\mathsf{Gr}_{5}(10);R)
.

displays the number of

and

of
\mathsf{Gr}_{12}(24)
.
